Integrated proteomic and metabolomic analysis reveals the mechanism of astragalus complanatus flavonoids against liver fibrosis in rats

Ruina Zhou, Yang Wang, Xinbo Shi, Shujie Peng, Xiaoni Kou — 2026-09-28 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fphar.2026.1949321

Objective Astragali Complanati Semen (ACS) is a traditional Chinese medicinal herb conventionally applied to tonify the liver and kidney. As the primary bioactive ingredients of ACS, Astragalus complanatus flavonoids (ACF) exert hepatoprotective and anti-fibrotic effects. This study aimed to elucidate the antifibrotic mechanisms of ACF through integrated proteomic and metabolomic analyses combined with experimental validation. Methods A CCl 4 -induced rat model of LF and TGF-β1-activated hepatic stellate cell (HSC) model were established to evaluate the antifibrotic effects of ACF. Integrated proteomic and metabolomic analyses were performed to identify differentially expressed proteins (DEPs), differentially expressed metabolites (DMs), and associated signaling pathways. Key molecular targets were validated using quantitative real-time PCR and Western blotting, while pharmacological inhibitors were employed to investigate the involvement of the FAK/AKT signaling pathway in ACF-mediated inhibition of HSC activation. Results Compared with the LF model group, ACF treatment significantly ameliorated liver function indicators, relieved histopathological injury and collagen deposition, decreased serum fibrosis markers, downregulated the protein levels of Col I, Col IV and α-SMA, and reduced oxidative stress and inflammatory response. Proteomic screening uncovered 401 differentially expressed proteins (DEPs), which were primarily enriched in extracellular matrix (ECM)–receptor interaction, focal adhesion and PI3K/AKT signaling pathways. Protein–protein interaction analysis further pinpointed FN1, CD44, ITGAV, FAK and AKT1 as core target proteins. In vitro experiments demonstrated that ACF blocked TGF-β1-triggered HSC activation, lowered the expression of α-SMA and ITGAV, and inhibited the phosphorylation of FAK and AKT. Pharmacological blockade of either FAK with PF-562271 or AKT with MK2206 strengthened the anti fibrogenic activity of ACF, verifying that the FAK/ AKT axis mediates the anti-HSC activation effect of ACF. Metabolomic analysis identified 108 differentially expressed metabolites (DMs), mainly enriched in glutathione metabolism, primary bile acid biosynthesis and carbohydrate metabolism. Integrated multi-omics analysis confirmed close correlations between DEPs and DMs within ECM–receptor interaction, PI3K/AKT signaling and primary bile acid biosynthesis pathways. Specifically, glycochenodeoxycholic acid, dulcitol and chenodeoxycholate showed strong correlations with coretarget proteins. Conclusion ACF achieves comprehensive anti-LF effects via suppressing FAK/AKT1 signaling to restrain HSC activation, while coordinately modulating pivotal metabolic pathways.

Cross-dataset transcriptomic analysis identifies oxidative Stress–inflammation gene networks modulated by nutrigenomic interventions in Parkinson’s disease

Masoumeh Rafiee, Faezeh Abaj, Reza Ghiasvand — 2026-09-28 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1007/s13721-026-00881-6

Abstract Inflammation and oxidative stress (OS) are key to Parkinson’s disease (PD). We performed a cross-dataset integrative transcriptomic analysis to identify OS- and inflammation-related hub genes consistently dysregulated in PD and to explore gene–compound relationships using nutrigenomic studies using publicly available datasets. Four GEO datasets (GSE7621, GSE20141, GSE20146, GSE49036) were analysed to identify differentially expressed genes (DEGs), which were intersected with GeneCards OS–inflammation gene sets. Functional enrichment analyses, including gene ontology (GO), pathway over-representation analysis (ORA), and protein-protein interaction (PPI) analysis, were used to identify key pathways and hub genes. Gene–food bioactive compound (FBC) association was explored by integrating PD signatures with nutrigenomic profiles from NutriGenomeDB. We identified 183 DEGs in PD, enriched in synaptic, dopaminergic, OS, and inflammatory pathways. Independent validation in a substantia nigra RNA-sequencing dataset (GSE168496) replicated 35 DEGs, all with concordant direction of expression and FDR < 0.05. Intersection analysis yielded 26 OS-inflammation-related genes and 10 central regulators, including TH , DDC , SNCA , LRRK2 , HSPB1 , and HSPA1B . Integration with nutrigenomic datasets revealed opposing-direction transcriptional patterns, with several FBC-associated signatures showing lower expression of stress-related genes and higher expression of dopaminergic markers such as TH , GCH1 , and DDC. Overall, this integrative analysis highlights validated OS–inflammation-associated transcriptional patterns in PD and identifies candidate diet–gene associations that warrant further experimental and clinical validation.

Genome-wide identification of aquaporin family in Camellia oleifera and their response characteristics to drought stress

Mengqi Lu, Kui Liu, Qizouhong He, Shaofeng Peng, Yanling Zeng — 2026-09-28 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fpls.2026.1959769

Aquaporins (AQPs) facilitate transmembrane water transport and are essential for plant growth, development, and stress adaptation. Camellia oleifera is an important woody edible oil tree. Drought stress frequently occurs during the rapid fruit expansion and maturation stages, and AQPs might mediate its drought response. However, the diversity, molecular characteristics and biological functions of AQPs in C. oleifera remain largely uncharacterized. Here, 166 CoAQPs were identified and classified into five subfamilies based on phylogenetic analysis. They were mainly localized to the plasma membrane and vacuole. Members within the same subfamily shared conserved gene structures and motif compositions. Segmental duplication was the main driving force for CoAQPs expansion. The promoter region of CoAQPs contained abundant cis-acting elements associated with light response, phytohormone response, stress response, and plant growth and development. Tissue-specific expression analysis revealed that 50 CoAQPs were highly expressed in leaves, whereas 27 CoAQPs showed differential expression in developing seeds. Drought stress induced significant physiological and biochemical responses in leaves and fruits of C. oleifera . Transcriptome data and real-time quantitative PCR analyses indicated CoAQPs extensively participated in drought response. This study will lay a theoretical foundation for exploring water metabolism mechanisms and screening gene resources for stress tolerance and quality improvement in C. oleifera .

Randomness Test of LightMAC-Based Pseudorandom Number Generator (PRNG) with NIST SP 800-22 Revision 1A Test

Bety Hayat Susanti, Wahyu Achmad Fadiel, Yeni Farida, Obrina Candra Briliyant — 2026-09-28 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.18860/cauchy.v11i2.43424

Random numbers are essential for ensuring the security and unpredictability of cryptographic systems; however, resource-constrained devices still lack effective lightweight random number generators. This gap leaves encryption, authentication, and key generation vulnerable, exposing critical infrastructure to adversarial exploitation. This study presents the development and statistical evaluation of a Pseudo-Random Number Generator (PRNG) based on the LightMAC construction instantiated with the PRESENT lightweight block cipher. The proposed PRNG operates by iteratively processing a fixed seed message through LightMAC-PRESENT with a varying counter. The randomness of the generated bit sequences was rigorously analyzed using the NIST SP 800-22 Revision 1A test suite. The evaluation encompassed multiple implementation configurations, including two seed message lengths ($|x|$) of 64 bits and 128 bits, two key lengths for the underlying PRESENT cipher of 80 bits and 128 bits, and three counter lengths of 8 bits, 16 bits, and 32 bits. The results demonstrate that the LightMAC-PRESENT-based PRNG satisfies the NIST statistical requirements under specific parameter configurations. For a 64-bit seed message, a 128-bit PRESENT key combined with an 8-bit or 16-bit counter successfully passes all tests. For a 128-bit seed message, the generator achieves compliance with an 80-bit key and a 128-bit key. The optimal configuration identified is a 128-bit seed message, an 80-bit key, and a 16-bit counter, which passes all tests across all key-pattern variations. These findings provide critical insights into the optimal parameters for secure, efficient, lightweight deterministic random bit generation in resource-constrained environments.

Sharp Calibration Depth and Robust Prediction in Sparse Uniform Hypergraphs

Adam Dzidowski — 2026-09-28 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23003198

Opis Głębokość kalibracji Sharp i solidna predykcja w rzadkich jednorodnych hipergrafach bada, ile informacji strukturalnych jest potrzebnych do tworzenia dokładnych i solidnych predykcji w rzadkich układach wyższego rzędu, gdy układ jest poddawany silnemu usuwaniu wierzchołków. Model jest nieujemnym, ważonym, jednorodnym hipergrafem zdefiniowanym na znanym, oznaczonym zbiorze wierzchołków, z zadaną górną granicą liczby hiperkrawędzi o dodatniej wadze. Silne usuwanie usuwa każdą hiperkrawędź, która przecina usunięty zbiór wierzchołków. Zadaniem predykcji jest określenie pozostałej reakcji na ograniczenie określonego punktu kotwiczącego po takich usunięciach. Głębokość kalibracji Sharp i Rob… The paper first determines the exact structural resolution needed for prediction. The available representation consists of labeled containment statistics of increasing order, where each statistic records the total weight of all hyperedges containing a given subset of vertices. The main predictive-threshold theorem identifies the least containment order that is sufficient for every allowed future deletion response. The result shows that this threshold is governed jointly by four mechanisms: the hyperedge order, the size of the complementary vertex set, the complexity of the future intervention, and sparsity of the hidden hypergraph. The result is sharp, and the lower bound is proved using explicit parity-trade constructions that produce different sparse hypergraphs with identical lower-order containment data but different post-deletion responses. The same threshold applies to both the nonnegative weighted class and the simple unweighted subclass. Sharp Calibration Depth and Rob… Sharp Calibration Depth and Rob… A second major part of the work treats calibration as an active identification problem. Instead of assuming that higher-order containment statistics can be measured directly, the paper considers controlled deletion experiments performed during calibration. For a fixed anchor size and a maximum number of deleted vertices per calibration experiment, the complete family of calibration responses is shown to contain exactly the same information as containment statistics up to a corresponding order. This establishes an exact information-theoretic equivalence between structural order and intervention depth. As a consequence, the paper derives the minimal calibration deletion depth required to guarantee exact prediction for a specified future task. Importantly, the result concerns the maximum depth of deletion used in an experiment, not the total number of measurements or experiments required. Sharp Calibration Depth and Rob… Sharp Calibration Depth and Rob… The paper then addresses robustness to calibration error. Exact identifiability alone does not guarantee that prediction remains stable under perturbations, so the analysis introduces a deterministic minimax formulation with coordinatewise bounded calibration errors. For the unbounded nonnegative weighted sparse class, the same calibration threshold that separates insufficient from sufficient exact information also separates two fundamentally different robustness regimes. Below the threshold, two admissible sparse models can produce identical calibration data while their future responses can be separated by an arbitrarily large amount through scaling, which makes the worst-case prediction error unbounded. At and above the threshold, the minimax prediction error becomes finite and proportional to the calibration error level. The paper provides an explicit sparsity-dependent upper bound on the corresponding stability constant, independent of the ambient number of vertices when the sparsity level is fixed. For the one-edge case, the stability constant is obtained exactly and equals one. Sharp Calibration Depth and Rob… Sharp Calibration Depth and Rob… A further contribution is the separation between nonlinear sparse prediction and linear decoding. The paper determines the least calibration depth at which an exact linear decoder can exist and shows that this linear threshold does not benefit from sparsity in the same way as arbitrary nonlinear prediction. Consequently, there are sparse regimes in which stable prediction is already possible from shallow calibration data, while every affine decoder still has infinite worst-case error on the unbounded weighted class. This distinction demonstrates that predictive sufficiency, structural reconstruction, and exact linear inversion are different notions and can require different amounts of information. Sharp Calibration Depth and Rob… The mathematical arguments combine Boolean-lattice Möbius inversion, inclusion-exclusion, uniform containment identities, sparse null-space reasoning, combinatorial trade constructions, optimal-recovery ideas, and finite-dimensional linear programming. The paper explicitly separates classical ingredients from the calibration-specific results developed in the manuscript. In particular, it does not claim novelty for Möbius inversion, inclusion matrices, combinatorial trades, or general sparse-identifiability principles; the claimed contribution lies in their combination for this specific intervention-prediction problem, the sharp calibration threshold, the robust minimax transition, the explicit sparse stability bound, and the separation from linear decoding. Sharp Calibration Depth and Rob… Praca omawia również szereg ograniczeń i warunków zakresu. Teoria kalibracji zakłada dostęp do pełnej, oznaczonej rodziny odpowiedzi kotwicowych/usunięć oraz powtarzalny system bazowy między interwencjami. Twierdzenie o odporności wykorzystuje deterministyczny błąd współrzędnych i nieograniczoną, nieujemną klasę modeli ważonych; w związku z tym wniosek o nieograniczonym błędzie poniżej progu nie rozciąga się automatycznie na hipergrafy o ograniczonej wadze ani proste hipergrafy. Wyraźna stała odporności jest celowo konserwatywna, a proponowany estymator optymalnego odzyskiwania jest skończony i konstruktywny, ale może wymagać wyliczenia wsparcia kombinatorycznego. Wyniki należy zatem interpretować jako precyzyjne stwierdzenia teorii informacji, a nie twierdzenia dotyczące wydajności próby, optymalnego projektu eksperymentu, dekodowania wielomianowego w czasie lub wydajności statystycznej w innych modelach szumu. Głębokość kalibracji ostrej i Rob… Dołączony pakiet do analizy reprodukcji zawiera źródło manuskryptu, skompilowany plik PDF, dokładne audyty obliczeniowe, dane wyjściowe w formacie maszynowym, jawne dowody dolnej granicy zakresu, wygenerowane rysunki i tabele, racjonalne certyfikaty primal-dualne, informacje o pochodzeniu oraz sumy kontrolne. Weryfikacja obliczeniowa niezależnie wylicza skończone klasy modeli, sprawdza tożsamości zawierania i usuwania, weryfikuje progi predykcyjne i klasyfikacje głębokości kalibracji, testuje głębsze dowody parzystości oraz weryfikuje zarejestrowane certyfikaty optymalizacji za pomocą dokładnej arytmetyki racjonalnej. Pakiet został zaprojektowany tak, aby kontrole matematyczne można było odtworzyć bez zewnętrznych zbiorów danych lub dostępu do internetu. Głębokość kalibracji Sharp i Rob… Głębokość kalibracji Sharp i Rob… Głównym celem pracy jest wyjaśnienie, kiedy ograniczone informacje strukturalne niższego rzędu są wystarczające do predykcji interwencji, kiedy aktywna kalibracja pozwala na odzyskanie brakujących informacji oraz kiedy dokładna wystarczalność predykcyjna zapewnia również solidność. Wyniki pokazują, że rzadkość może zmniejszyć ilość informacji potrzebnych do predykcji nieliniowej, że kontrolowane interwencje można interpretować jako pomiary struktury wyższego rzędu, a solidność predykcji może stać się możliwa tuż przed dokładną inwersją liniową.

Tinnitus outcomes after cochlear implantation in single-sided deafness: an exploratory prospective multicenter study

Jae-Jin Song, Yeonjoo Choi, Yehree Kim, Jun Ho Lee, Hong Ju Park — 2026-09-28 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fneur.2026.1970581

Tinnitus is thought to be suppressed through partial reversal of auditory deafferentation via cochlear implantation (CI). By stimulating the deafferented auditory pathway, multiple studies have revealed that tinnitus is significantly suppressed in 66–92% of patients undergoing CI. This study aimed to evaluate the efficacy of CI as a treatment for tinnitus and to investigate post-CI audiologic outcomes in patients with single-sided deafness (SSD). From November 2018 to July 2020, 10 patients who were diagnosed with SSD and suffering from tinnitus were prospectively recruited at three tertiary referral hospitals. Participants in this study agreed to undergo CI as a treatment for tinnitus. Postoperative aided pure tone audiometry (PTA) and word recognition score (WRS) were measured. Questionnaires related to tinnitus, such as the tinnitus handicap inventory (THI) and visual analogue scale (VAS) scores including loudness, distress, and perception, were administered. Additionally, a tinnitogram was obtained for loudness and pitch matching. Audiological outcomes of CI were analyzed in conjunction with the results of pitch-matching from the tinnitogram. All 10 patients completed device-on evaluations, and 9 patients completed device-off evaluations. THI scores showed statistically significant improvement 3 months after CI in both the device-on and device-off conditions (preoperative: 78; device on: 38, p = 0.027; device off: 50, p = 0.049). VAS scores also demonstrated significant improvement after CI (preoperative: 8.2; device on: 3.3, p = 0.011; device off: 6.5, p = 0.012). Four patients demonstrated changes in psychoacoustic tinnitus features after CI: 2 changed to lower frequency, 1 changed to higher frequency, and 1 patient’s tinnitus changed from white noise to a specific identifiable frequency. There was no significant tendency for threshold (T-level and C-level) changes in pitch-matching frequency. In line with previous literature, this exploratory multicenter study demonstrated improvement in tinnitus loudness and distress after CI in patients with single-sided deafness and tinnitus, with a tendency for greater reduction in tinnitus intensity observed when the device was activated. Additionally, approximately half of the patients showed changes in the psychoacoustic characteristics of tinnitus after CI. Given the small sample size and the absence of a non-implanted control group, these findings should be regarded as exploratory and hypothesis-generating, and warrant confirmation in adequately powered controlled trials.

A methodological approach to improve extracellular staphylococcal clearance in internalization assays using bone-associated host-cell models of orthopedic infections

Stefania Mamberti, Giada Meogrossi, Elena Vecchi, Vincenzo Pennone, Arianna Barbara Lovati — 2026-09-28 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fcimb.2026.1945457

Orthopedic implant-related infections are a major cause of prosthesis failure and are associated with biofilm formation, antimicrobial tolerance, and bacterial persistence. Bacterial internalization into bone-associated host cells may also contribute to chronic or recurrent infection. However, non-standardized extracellular bacterial clearance, multiplicity-of-infection definition, and data normalization limit assay interpretation and comparability. This study aimed to establish a stepwise protocol with a structured strategy to obtain the extracellular staphylococcal clearance allowing for the unbiased quantification of host-cell internalized bacteria through identification and prevention of major methodological pitfalls. Cell-associated bacteria from host-cell lysates were a secondary endpoint. Methicillin-resistant S. aureus USA300 and S. epidermidis RP62A were used as model pathogens, whereas MG-63 osteosarcoma cells and human bone-marrow mesenchymal stromal cells served as host-cell models. Vancomycin and lysostaphin were first evaluated in the absence of host cells with bacterial inocula simulating multiplicities of infection (MOI) of 50 and 100. Clearance was more effective and consistent at the lower inoculum. Accordingly, 25 µg/mL lysostaphin for 30 min and 100 µg/mL vancomycin for 60 min were tested individually or sequentially after a 2-h infection at an MOI of 50. Vancomycin alone resulted in the highest residual extracellular bacterial recovery, precluding interpretation of CFU recovered from cell lysates. Lysostaphin-containing protocols reduced viable CFU recovered from the final treatment medium to near or below the assay detection limit. S. epidermidis recovery from cell lysates was negligible after lysostaphin-containing treatments. Phase-contrast microscopy monitored host-cell morphology, whereas confocal microscopy supported intracellular bacterial localization. These findings indicate that antimicrobial treatments should be validated within the bacteria–host-cell system rather than selected solely from cell-free assays. The framework integrates strain-specific antimicrobial testing, inoculum selection, verification of extracellular clearance, normalization to host-cell number, and microscopy-based assessment, improving the rigor and comparability of in vitro studies of staphylococcal interactions with bone-associated cells.

Peste des Petits Ruminants in Sheep

Dr. J. Shiva Jyothi M.V.Sc., Ph.D, — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22998803

Peste des petits ruminants, commonly known as PPR or sheep and goat plague, is one of the most important viral diseases affecting small ruminants. The disease is highly contagious and can spread rapidly through sheep and goat populations, causing severe illness, high mortality and substantial economic losses. Although goats are often severely affected, sheep are also highly susceptible and can develop serious disease, particularly when a susceptible flock is exposed to the virus for the first time. PPR is much more than an animal disease. For millions of smallholder farmers, sheep and goats represent savings, food, income and financial security. Losses caused by PPR can therefore directly affect household livelihoods and food security. WOAH and FAO estimate that more than 330 million people worldwide depend on small ruminants, while annual economic losses associated with PPR can reach approximately USD 2.1 billion. The good news is that PPR is considered an excellent candidate for global eradication. An effective vaccine is available, the virus has a relatively short infectious period, there is no recognized carrier state and there is no established long-term reservoir outside susceptible animal populations. FAO and WOAH have therefore set 2030 as the target for global eradication of PPR.

Association of vaccination status and vaccine type with quality of life in hospitalized COVID-19 patients

Muhammed Fatih Baran, Durmuş Ali Aslanlar, Mustafa Önder Gönen — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.66235/kumj.1866154

Aims: The aim of this study is to investigate whether vaccination, as one of the most critical preventive measures against infectious diseases, has an impact on the quality of life post-infection. Additionally, it aims to evaluate the effects of different vaccine types used in the prevention of COVID-19 on post-infection quality of life.Methods: This single-center observational study included adults hospitalized with COVID-19 who were evaluated at a follow-up center one month after discharge. Participants were assessed at two visits one month apart. Quality of life was evaluated using the SF-12 Quality of Life Scale, and findings were compared according to vaccination status and vaccine type.Results: The study was completed with 98 participants aged 28–87 years, with a mean age of 56±13.0 years. While physical health scores did not differ significantly between the two follow-ups in the unvaccinated group, a significant improvement was observed among vaccinated participants (p=0.027). Among the vaccinated group, physical health scores showed no significant difference between the two follow-ups for mRNA vaccine recipients, but a significant improvement was observed for those who had received inactivated vaccines (p=0.015). The number of complaints decreased significantly in both vaccine groups. Positive correlations were found between the values of the SF-12 scale across the two follow-ups (p<0.05). Conversely, negative correlations were observed between SF-12 scores and the number of complaints at both follow-ups (p<0.001). A positive correlation was also noted between the number of complaints across the two follow-ups (p<0.001).Conclusion: Vaccination was associated with improvement in some quality-of-life parameters during follow-up after hospitalization for COVID-19. Differences between vaccine types should be interpreted cautiously and confirmed in larger studies.

Does postpartum welfare clinic utilization impact child survival? Evidence from a cross-sectional study of postnatal healthcare determinants in Ghana.

Samuel Amon — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.34005159

In Ghana, the principal vehicle for operationalizing PHC and advancing UHC is the Community-Based Health Planning and Services (CHPS) initiative, introduced nationwide to bring essential health services directly to rural and underserved communities. Within this framework, Child Welfare Clinics (CWCs) - operating both as static facility units and routine mobile outreach sessions - serve as the primary post-natal platforms for routine child healthcare monitoring. CWCs deliver a comprehensive package of essential postnatal and early childhood services, including growth monitoring, preventive nutritional counselling, routine infant immunizations (such as DTP, Polio, Hepatitis B, Measles, and Yellow Fever), and micronutrient supplementation (e.g., Vitamin A)Although literature extensively documents the theoretical benefits of preventive child healthcare, empirical evidence evaluating whether sustained, routine postpartum child welfare clinic utilization directly translates into improved longitudinal under-five survival remains rare. Many studies focus narrowly on single clinical interventions, such as specific routine vaccines or exclusive breastfeeding, without assessing the cumulative impact of overall postnatal welfare clinic attendance patterns and check frequencies relative to structural and socioeconomic determinants. Furthermore, out-of-pocket indirect expenses, spatial distance to health centers, and maternal socioeconomic status continue to act as major confounding drivers of both service utilization and child survival outcomes across Ghanaian communities. Understanding whether, and to what extent, postpartum CWC utilization reduces the hazard of under-five mortality is essential for refining national PHC policies and accelerating progress toward SDG 3.2. Therefore, this study aimed to investigate the impact of postpartum CWC utilization and postnatal healthcare determinants on under-five child survival, utilizing retrospective cross-sectional data from two underserved (Volta and Northern) regions in Ghana.

Development of a cross-protective subunit cocktail vaccine against diverse serotypes of Glaesserella parasuis infection in pigs

Fengyang Li, Yan Gong, Ziheng Li, Zhen Wang, Zengshuai Wu — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s13567-026-01846-3

Abstract Glässer’s disease, caused by Glaesserella parasuis (GPS), causes substantial economic losses in the swine industry worldwide. This situation is largely attributable to the poor cross-protective efficacy of currently available vaccines. In this study, a multi-antigen cocktail subunit vaccine against GPS infection was developed. In silico antigenicity analysis, the immunoprotective efficacy, and antibody titers of 14 GPS antigens were assessed in a murine model. Finally, VacJ, PtsG, and GAPDH were identified as the component of the cocktail vaccine, with Gel-01 as the optimal adjuvant. Immunization of the cocktail vaccine induced robust humoral and cellular immune responses. The vaccine conferred effective cross-protection against lethal-dose GPS4 and GPS5 infections in mice. As supported by a pilot study, it also demonstrated favorable immunoprotective efficacy against GPS infection in piglets. Furthermore, immune sera from vaccinated mice significantly inhibited the growth of GPS4 and GPS5. Overall, these results suggested that the subunit cocktail vaccine established in this study exhibits great potential for preventing GPS infection under experimental conditions, and further large‑scale pig trials are still required to validate its practical applicability in field production.

Characterising host responses to multi-drug resistant Salmonella enterica 4,[5],12:i:-, sequence type 34 monophasic isolates

Sahampath Upekha Hettiarachchi — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.26180/34005612.v1

This project studied a type of Salmonella called ST34 monophasic Salmonella. Unlike typical Salmonella that causes short-lived food poisoning, this strain can spread beyond the gut, cause severe illness, and is hard to treat because it is resistant to antibiotics. Despite this, very little is known about how it does this. This study identified two human proteins that ST34 monophasic Salmonella alter during infection, which could help explain how it causes invasive disease. These findings improve our understanding of this dangerous strain and may support future treatments or vaccine development.

Effect of HPV Vaccine Introduction at 9-10 years: A Randomized Clinical Trial

Allison Kempe, Sitaram Vangala, Christina Albertin, Sean O’Leary, Beaty Brenda — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.09.24.26363898

Importance: In the US, Human papillomavirus (HPV) related cancers cause significant morbidity and mortality, yet HPV vaccine is underutilized. Vaccination is recommended routinely at age 11-12 years but can be given at 9 years. There is controversy about whether earlier introduction would increase completion of the HPV series before by age 13 years or whether it might decrease rates of delivery of other routinely recommended vaccines at 11-12-years [quadrivalent meningococcal conjugate (MenACWY) vaccine, and tetanus-diptheria-acellular pertussis (Tdap) vaccine]. Objective: To determine whether introduction of the HPV vaccine at ages 9-10 versus 11-12 years, results in earlier median age of series completion (primary outcome) and higher rates of initiation and completion by age 13 years. We also examined the effect of earlier introduction of HPV on rates of Tdap and MenACWY initiation and completion by age 13 years. Design: Two-arm cluster-randomized trial involving 31 pediatric practices in Colorado and California between 10/2021-4/2026. Practices were randomized using covariate constrained randomization within each state to Intervention (introduction of HPV vaccination at ages 9-10) or Control (introduction at ages 11-12). Both study groups received similar annual training about best practices for HPV vaccination with recommended initiation age as the only difference. Results: Age at HPV series completion was substantially earlier in the Intervention group (median 12.2 v. 13.3 years; adjusted hazard ratio [aHR] 2.37; 95% CI 1.94-2.89). Key secondary outcomes were also improved in the intervention group: age at series initiation (median 11.0 v. 11.8 years; aHR 2.19; 95% CI 1.84-2.62), series completion by 13 years (61.6% v. 45.1%; RR 1.37; 95% CI 1.04-1.74), and vaccine initiation by age 13 years (77.1% v. 67.0%; RR 1.15; 95% CI 1.00-1.33). The intervention group was more likely to receive Tdap (aHR 1.47;95%CI 1.13, 1.92) during the trial; receipt of both vaccines by age 13 years did not differ. Conclusions: Earlier introduction of HPV vaccination resulted in earlier age of series completion, and more adolescents initiating and completing the series by age 13 without decreasing rates of other routinely recommended vaccines. Our findings support a preferential recommendation at 9-10 years. Trial registration number: NCT04722822 ClinicalTrial.gov

VaxGuard: Vaccine Cold Chain Monitor for Community Health Workers

Won Ho Lee — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22984937

VaxGuard is Module 4 of the EpiCalc Suite: a vaccine cold chain monitoring tool for Community Health Workers assessing vaccine potency when cold chain integrity is uncertain. It implements the WHO/FDA Arrhenius-based Mean Kinetic Temperature (MKT) formula (ICH Q1E guideline) to compute residual potency and a three-tier use/ conditional/discard decision, with presets for five WHO EPI vaccines (OPV, BCG, Measles, DTP/Penta, HepB) and Vaccine Vial Monitor (VVM) cross-checking. The interface is fully localized in English, French, Swahili, and Korean. The app runs entirely client-side as an installable, offline-capable Progressive Web App, with no data collection.

Knowledge and Attitudes towards Human Papillomavirus and HPV vaccination among girls aged 9-14 years in Lira City West, Northern Uganda: A cross-Sectional Study

Beth Namukwana, Naume Ayoo, Caroline Nabasirye, Deborah Namutebi, Vicky Achayo — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.63363/aijfr.2026.v07i05.7941

Background: Human papillomavirus (HPV) is the necessary cause of nearly all cervical cancers and remains an important public-health problem, particularly in low- and middle-income countries. Vaccination before exposure to HPV is an effective primary prevention strategy. This study assessed knowledge of HPV and HPV vaccination and attitudes towards HPV vaccination among girls aged 9–14 years attending selected primary schools in Lira City West Division, Northern Uganda. Methods: A quantitative cross-sectional study was conducted among 398 girls aged 9–14 years from three high-enrolment primary schools. Participants were stratified by school and class and selected systematically within strata. Data were collected using an interviewer-administered structured questionnaire in English or Leb Lango. Sociodemographic characteristics, individual knowledge items and five-point Likert attitude items were analysed descriptively using frequencies and percentages in IBM SPSS Statistics version 27. Results: Only 26.4% of participants had heard of HPV and 15.6% identified HPV as a virus associated with cervical cancer. When asked about transmission, 65.8% selected sexual contact and 14.6% selected skin-to-skin contact. Awareness of the HPV vaccine was higher, with 76.9% having heard of it and 86.7% indicating that it can prevent cervical cancer. Favourable attitudes were common: 77.9% intended to receive the vaccine, 79.4% perceived it as safe, 78.6% considered vaccination important and 77.9% would recommend it to friends. Parents or guardians influenced vaccination views among 75.1% of participants. However, 95.0% disagreed or strongly disagreed that sufficient HPV-vaccine information was available to them. Conclusion: Girls in the selected schools were generally receptive to HPV vaccination but had limited understanding of HPV infection and its relationship with cervical cancer. The findings show a mismatch between relatively high vaccine awareness and favourable attitudes, and limited HPV knowledge alongside perceived insufficiency of information. School-based vaccination should be accompanied by repeated, age-appropriate education involving girls, parents and health workers.

Technologies Révolutionnaires pour Étendre l'Espérance de Vie et Personnaliser la Médecine

Xavier Pillet — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23002289

Abstract ENThis document, produced with the assistance of ChatGPT o3 and GPT-5 Thinking, is released under the Apache 2.0 license. It consolidates 100 enabling embodiments spanning gold nanomedicine (photothermal ablation, CRISPR-Gold delivery, nanozymes, theranostics), whole-body sensing (MRI, OPM/EEG, exposome), imaging/reconstruction (photoacoustics, DECT, PCD-CT), AI (digital twins, safety-constrained bandits/RL, federated/split learning, HE/TEE privacy), closed-loop neuromodulation and perfusion, GMP manufacturing/QA, packaging and traceability. Each item includes reproducible parameters (materials, device specs, acquisition, dosing, QA/PAT) and at least one IPC and one CPC classification, enabling practitioners to implement and thereby pre-empt narrow patents. A scoping review of artificial intelligence applications for mpox preparedness, prediction, prevention, and surveillance in the era of emerging epidemics

Mohan Bilikallahalli Sannathimmappa, Nambiar Vinod, Shahenaz Satti — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s12982-026-03038-y

The 2022–2024 global resurgence of mpox (formerly monkeypox) and the successive declarations of a Public Health Emergency of International Concern have exposed persistent weaknesses in conventional disease preparedness, prediction, prevention, and surveillance. Artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning (ML) offer scalable tools to strengthen each of these pillars, yet the evidence remains fragmented across diagnostic imaging, epidemiological forecasting, genomic analytics, digital infodemiology, and therapeutic discovery. To systematically map the scope, methods, performance, and translational maturity of AI applications across the mpox epidemic continuum, and to identify research gaps that should guide future preparedness for emerging epidemics. We conducted a scoping review following the PRISMA Extension for Scoping Reviews (PRISMA-ScR) framework. PubMed, Scopus, Web of Science, IEEE Xplore, and Embase were searched from database inception to the final search date of 30 September 2025. Eligible records were concentrated between 2018 and 2025. After de-duplication and two-stage screening performed by a single reviewer, 48 studies meeting predefined eligibility criteria were charted and synthesized under four thematic domains: diagnosis and detection, prediction and forecasting, surveillance and digital epidemiology, and drug and vaccine discovery. Deep-learning image classifiers dominated the diagnostic literature, with reported accuracies and area-under-the-curve values ranging from approximately 0.83 to 0.99 for distinguishing mpox skin lesions from clinical mimics. Time-series and neural-network models (ARIMA, LSTM, GRU, and ensemble sub-epidemic frameworks) produced credible short-term case forecasts, while natural-language-processing pipelines tracked public sentiment and misinformation in near real time. AI-assisted genomic surveillance, wastewater monitoring, and structure-based drug and epitope discovery represented rapidly emerging but less mature domains. Reported performance metrics were author-reported values obtained under heterogeneous datasets and validation protocols, and were not pooled. Across studies, small and imbalanced datasets, limited external validation, and weak clinical integration were recurrently reported by the included primary studies. AI shows promise in augmenting mpox preparedness, prediction, prevention, and surveillance, but most tools remain at a proof-of-concept stage. Prospective validation, equitable data governance, explainability, and integration into public-health workflows are prerequisites for real-world impact in future epidemics.

Immune Checkpoint Inhibitor as a Fifth Component of Lipid Nanoparticles Enables Lymph Node‐Targeting and Augments mRNA Vaccine Efficacy

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Minki Ha, Jaehyun Kim, Seyoung Kim, Gonna Somu Naidu, Sungchan Cho — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1002/advs.77923

ABSTRACT mRNA‐encapsulated lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) have been used to develop vaccines and therapeutics since the COVID‐19 pandemic, and clinical studies have demonstrated therapeutic benefits of mRNA vaccines for various cancers. However, current LNP formulations still require functional modifications to tailor them for therapeutic and vaccine applications. Recently, fifth component‐incorporated LNPs were developed to finely tune their functionality, including immunogenicity and organ‐targeting ability. Here, biphenyl‐structured amphiphilic PDL1 inhibitors are employed as a fifth component to modify LNPs. Amphiphilic PDL1 inhibitor‐incorporated, ALC0315‐based LNPs show higher lymph node targeting, with a 6.94‐fold increase in mRNA expression in the inguinal lymph node, thereby improving lymph node expansion and immune cell infiltration and, consequently, inducing greater mRNA vaccine efficacy. Furthermore, a consistent lymph node‐targeting effect of the fifth component‐containing LNP was observed across studies using the SM102‐based LNP formulation, suggesting its universality. In conclusion, this study demonstrates that PDL1 inhibitor‐incorporated fifth‐component LNP serves as a next‐generation platform for mRNA vaccines.

Cationic surfactant-dependent enhancement of antigen-specific cellular immunity by a biodegradable nanoparticle vaccine

Jorge Huete‐Carrasco, Jeremy Aboagye, Dorothée Neple, McClean Siobhan, Thomas Courant — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.vaccine.2026.129195

Infectious diseases remain a leading cause of morbidity and mortality worldwide and while antimicrobials play a crucial role in controlling infections, the rise of antimicrobial resistance (AMR) is a major global threat. Vaccination is key to prevent and treat diseases caused by resistant pathogens and there is a particular need for vaccines that induce strong T cell-mediated immunity. Nanoparticles comprised of biodegradable polymers, such as poly (lactic- co -glycolic acid) (PLGA), have shown promise due to their biocompatibility, controlled degradation, and capacity for surface modification. A particle size in the 50–60 nm range is beneficial to induce CD8 + T cell and Th1-biased responses. However, manufacturing such small PLGA nanoparticles with traditional approaches such as emulsification/solvent evaporation is challenging. Here, we addressed this challenge and formulated 50 nm polymeric nanoparticles using a modified nanoprecipitation method. We investigated the effect of a cationic surfactant in shaping adaptive immune responses. Nanoparticles stabilized using DOTAP and with a (50:50) PLA:PGA ratio were particularly effective in enhancing antigen-specific CD8 + T cells, CD4 + Th1 responses, and antigen-specific serum IgG titres. Notably, surfactant-free nanoparticles failed to elicit T cell responses and were less effective at enhancing antigen-specific antibodies, highlighting the crucial role of the cationic surfactant in promoting vaccine-induced adaptive immune responses. These findings were validated using ovalbumin (OVA) and Burkholderia pseudomallei outer membrane protein (BpOmpW) as antigens. Given that B. pseudomallei shows both intrinsic and acquired antibiotic resistance, this work underscores the potential of biodegradable polymeric nanoparticles as a promising platform for next-generation vaccines against AMR pathogens.

🏭 NANOMETROLOGY, NANOMANUFACTURING & DIGITAL SCALE-UP AT THE LIMIT Measurement Science, Process Windows, Yield Engineering, Digital Twins, and the Emerging Science of Nanoscale State Reproducibility

33 — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23001246

🏭 NANOMETROLOGY, NANOMANUFACTURING & DIGITAL SCALE-UP AT THE LIMIT Measurement Science, Process Windows, Yield Engineering, Digital Twins, and the Emerging Science of Nanoscale State Reproducibility Can a nanoscale state be measured, reproduced, and preserved from one specimen to billions of products? This flagship research volume explores one of the most important transitions in nanotechnology: the transition from demonstrating an extraordinary nanoscale effect once to reproducing that effect reliably across laboratories, production lines, devices, batches, and eventually billions of manufactured products. A beautiful specimen is not yet a process. A successful experiment is not yet reproducibility. A repeated recipe is not necessarily a repeated nanoscale state. And a laboratory result is not yet a manufacturing technology. At the nanoscale, extraordinarily small variations in structure, surface chemistry, interfaces, defects, contamination, temperature, precursor history, processing conditions, measurement method, and environmental exposure can produce disproportionately large changes in performance. This creates a fundamental engineering problem: How do we define what must remain the same when the object being manufactured is too small, complex, dynamic, or fragile to be described by composition alone? This book develops that problem across a broad research landscape including: atomic layer deposition,CVD and PVD,ultrathin films,tribological nanocoatings,barrier and protective coatings,electron microscopy,scanning probe microscopy,spectroscopy,tomography,atom-probe reconstruction,in situ characterization,operando measurement,measurement uncertainty,correlative microscopy,batch-to-batch variation,colloidal synthesis,lithographic nanostructures,roll-to-roll processing,printing,additive nanomanufacturing,statistical process control,process windows,yield engineering,defect inspection,accelerated reliability,digital manufacturing twins,reference materials,and scale-up invariants. The central idea of the book is that a nanoscale material cannot be separated completely from the way it is measured and manufactured. Measurement is not merely observation. At sufficiently small scales, measurement may influence the state being measured. Electron beams can damage samples. Scanning probes can deform surfaces. Environmental conditions can change interfaces. Preparation methods can alter chemistry. Sampling can hide rare but important defects. Reconstruction algorithms can introduce interpretation uncertainty. The observed nanoscale state is therefore coupled to: the specimen,the instrument,the measurement model,the environment,the operator,the calibration chain,and the data-processing pathway. This creates a deeper principle: Metrology is part of nanoscale engineering. A nanoscale state cannot become industrially meaningful unless different laboratories and production systems can identify that state with sufficiently comparable meaning. This requires: traceability,reference materials,uncertainty budgets,cross-laboratory reproducibility,orthogonal measurements,calibration,metadata continuity,and clearly defined measurands. The question is no longer simply: What did the microscope see? It becomes: What physical state does the measurement represent, how uncertain is that representation, and can another laboratory recover the same conclusion? Manufacturing introduces another major challenge. A laboratory process may operate within an extremely narrow window. Small changes in temperature,pressure,precursor purity,mixing time,residence time,surface preparation,deposition rate,web speed,ink rheology,annealing,or environmental conditions may shift the resulting nanoscale state. The engineering objective therefore cannot be limited to discovering the single point of maximum performance. The more important objective is often discovering a robust process window. A useful process window is a region in which: function,yield,measurement confidence,manufacturing tolerance,cost,reliability,and environmental control can coexist. This changes optimization. Instead of asking: Where is the highest possible performance? the book repeatedly asks: Where is the broadest region in which useful performance remains reproducible? This distinction becomes critical during scale-up. Scaling a nanoscale process is not simply a matter of making more material. Reactor geometry changes. Heat transfer changes. Mixing changes. Residence-time distributions change. Tool drift accumulates. Defect statistics change. Inspection becomes harder. Sampling becomes more important. Production speed creates new constraints. The engineering challenge is therefore to identify scale-up invariants: Which physical relationships must remain preserved as the process becomes larger, faster, or more distributed? This leads to a central translation chain: Specimen→Measurement→State Definition→Process Window→Reproduction→Yield→Reliability→Scale Yield becomes especially important. At the nanoscale, average performance can be misleading. A process may produce excellent average values while still generating rare catastrophic defects. A production system must therefore understand: distributions,tails,outliers,spatial variation,batch variation,tool variation,operator variation,and temporal drift. This transforms defect inspection from a final quality-control step into part of the architecture of manufacturing itself. The book also develops the role of statistical process control for nanotechnology. When manufacturing states are defined through multiple coupled variables, monitoring a single metric may not be enough. A production system may need to continuously estimate: structural state,surface state,interface state,defect state,process history,measurement confidence,and functional performance. This creates the possibility of state-aware manufacturing. Instead of controlling only machine settings, future nanomanufacturing systems may control inferred material states. Digital twins become especially important in this transition. A digital manufacturing twin can integrate: process data,metrology,material-state models,equipment history,sensor streams,yield data,failure observations,and predictive simulation. The purpose of the twin is not merely to create a digital copy of a factory. Its deeper role is to maintain continuity between: what was intended,what was manufactured,what was measured,and what the material actually became. This opens the possibility of closed-loop nanoscale manufacturing: Measure→Infer State→Compare→Correct→Manufacture→Measure Again But digital twins cannot replace physical evidence. A model can help identify drift. It can help locate process windows. It can help predict yield loss. It can help prioritize inspection. It can help explore counterfactual process changes. But the model itself must remain calibrated against reality. A digital twin that drifts away from the physical process becomes another source of uncertainty. For this reason, the book repeatedly connects digital modeling with traceable metrology and real-world validation. The volume also examines accelerated reliability. A nanoscale system may initially satisfy every specification and still fail because its state evolves over time. Interfaces react. Defects migrate. Coatings delaminate. Surfaces oxidize. Particles aggregate. Grains grow. Contamination accumulates. Stress redistributes. Repeated cycles alter the material. Reliability therefore requires more than measuring the initial state. It requires understanding state trajectories. This leads to another central question: Can we predict when a nanoscale state will stop being the state we originally manufactured? The book develops this question into an emerging research direction: Nanoscale State Reproducibility Engineering. Nanoscale State Reproducibility Engineering asks how a nanoscale state can be: defined,measured,reconstructed,reproduced,preserved,monitored,scaled,and transferred without losing the mechanisms responsible for its useful function. Its core logic is: State Definition→Measurement→Uncertainty→Reproduction→Process Control→Yield→Reliability→Digital Twin→Scale This discipline connects nanoscience with manufacturing science. It asks not merely whether a nanoscale phenomenon exists. It asks whether that phenomenon can become dependable technology. The book repeatedly explores questions such as: What exactly constitutes the nanoscale state that must be reproduced? Which variables are essential and which are incidental? How much measurement uncertainty can a process tolerate? Can different instruments produce equivalent interpretations? How should cross-laboratory reproducibility be defined? Which defects control yield? How should rare failure-driving features be sampled? What must remain invariant during 100×, 1,000×, or million-fold scale-up? Can digital twins detect process drift before products fail? How should nanoscale reference materials be designed? Can operando measurements distinguish genuine evolution from measurement-induced change? Can billions of manufactured structures retain the same functional state as the original laboratory specimen? And when does reproducibility become sufficient for industrial trust? Designed as a large-scale Living Interactive research volume, the book contains 200 Research Gates. Each Gate represents an unresolved research space rather than a final answer. The Gates extend into questions involving: nanometrology,uncertainty budgets,cross-laboratory reproducibility,correlative microscopy,measurement perturbation,reference materials,operando characterization,batch variation,process windows,yield engineering,statistical process control,defect inspection,roll-to-roll manufacturing,atomic layer processing,digital manufacturing twins,accelerated reliability,scale-up invar

Monotonicity-constrained physics-informed neural networks for concrete compressive strength prediction

Anik Mazumder, Mahathir Mohammad Bappy, Bhaba R. Sarker — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1007/s41062-026-02981-8

Abstract Accurate prediction of concrete compressive strength is critical for mix design optimization, quality control, and reliable decision-making in modern construction. While data-driven machine learning models have demonstrated strong predictive capability, most existing approaches treat concrete behavior as a purely statistical problem, often neglecting well-established physical relationships among mix constituents and curing conditions. This limitation can lead to physically inconsistent predictions and reduced interpretability, hindering adoption in engineering practice. To address these gaps, this study proposes a new physics-informed neural network (PINN) framework for concrete compressive strength prediction that explicitly embeds domain knowledge into the learning process. The model utilizes eight fundamental mix-design variables and curing age, integrating a conventional data-driven cross-entropy loss with physics-based gradient constraints that enforce known monotonic trends, including strength reduction with increasing water content and strength enhancement with increasing binder content and curing age. A structured hyperparameter search is conducted to identify an optimal network architecture comprising two hidden layers with rectified linear unit activation, dropout regularization, and the Adam optimizer. The optimized PINN achieved a validation accuracy of 0.975 and an independent test accuracy of 0.978. Across the three strength classes, precision ranged from 0.93 to 0.99, recall from 0.97 to 1.00, and F1-score from 0.97 to 0.98. The continuous expected-strength output achieved an $${R}^{2}$$ of 0.834, an MAE of 5.481 MPa, and an RMSE of 6.758 MPa. Gradient-based evaluation further showed a nonpositive sensitivity to water and nonnegative sensitivities to curing age and the constrained binder components. These results demonstrate that embedding physics-based constraints enhances both predictive performance and interpretability, positioning the proposed PINN framework as a robust and practical tool for mix optimization, quality assurance, and decision support in precast concrete manufacturing.

⚛ NANOSCALE PHYSICS & SIZE-DEPENDENT TRANSPORT AT THE LIMIT Quantum Confinement, Size Effects, Statistical Heterogeneity, and Mechanical, Thermal, Electrical, and Optical Transport Across Nanoscale Regimes

33 — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22999625

⚛️ NANOSCALE PHYSICS & SIZE-DEPENDENT TRANSPORT AT THE LIMIT Quantum Confinement, Size Effects, Statistical Heterogeneity, and Mechanical, Thermal, Electrical, and Optical Transport Across Nanoscale Regimes When does smaller become physically different, rather than merely smaller? This flagship research volume explores one of the foundational questions of nanoscience: At what point does reducing size stop being a geometric change and begin to alter the governing physics of matter? At the nanoscale, dimensions can approach characteristic physical lengths such as mean free paths, diffusion lengths, screening lengths, grain sizes, defect spacings, interaction ranges, and quantum confinement lengths. When this happens, familiar bulk behavior may no longer remain sufficient. Surfaces can dominate volume. Boundaries can control transport. Discrete energy states can emerge. Interfaces can become primary resistance elements. Mechanical deformation mechanisms can change. Thermal, electrical, and optical responses can become strongly size-dependent. And the statistical distribution of nanoscale objects may become as important as the properties of any single object. This book develops these transitions as a coherent research framework. Its central objective is not merely to describe small materials. It is to identify the boundaries between physical regimes. The research landscape includes: nanoscale design grammar,surface-to-volume dominance,quantum confinement,statistical heterogeneity,single-particle versus ensemble behavior,mechanical size effects,inverse Hall–Petch regimes,ballistic–diffusive crossover,boundary scattering,nanoscale thermal transport,electrical transport under dimensional confinement,optical size effects,plasmonic response,size-dependent phase stability,characteristic-length competition,and quantum-to-classical transition regimes. A central principle of the book is that nanoscale behavior cannot be interpreted through size alone. The relevant state is coupled. A nanoscale system may depend simultaneously on: size,shape,surface state,interface density,defect population,composition,hierarchy,processing history,measurement context,and environmental conditions. The same nominal material can therefore occupy very different physical states. A useful nanoscale theory must explain not only what property changes, but why the governing mechanism changes, which characteristic length becomes dominant, how the transition can be measured, and whether the effect survives realistic variation. The book repeatedly asks: Which length scale is controlling the observed behavior? Where does one physical regime give way to another? Is the measured effect intrinsic to the material, or created by interfaces, defects, processing, or measurement artifacts? How broad is the transition region? Can the effect be reproduced across particles, devices, batches, laboratories, and manufacturing scales? What happens when the nanoscale state drifts over time? And which apparently superior nanoscale effects disappear when reliability and scale-up are included? One major theme is quantum confinement. As structural dimensions approach relevant quantum length scales, electronic and optical states may change in ways that cannot be described simply by scaling down bulk matter. But confinement is never isolated. Surface states, dielectric environment, carrier density, defect structure, and size distribution can all alter the observed response. The research problem is therefore not simply: Does quantum confinement occur? It is: Under what conditions does confinement become the dominant physical regime, and how can that regime be preserved, measured, and engineered? A second major theme is transport. At sufficiently small dimensions, thermal and electrical transport may move between diffusive, quasi-ballistic, ballistic, interface-limited, and boundary-scattering regimes. The governing physics may depend on the relationship between device dimensions and characteristic transport lengths. This creates a deeper engineering question: Can transport regimes themselves become design variables? Mechanical behavior creates a similar challenge. Grain size, dislocation source length, interface density, strain rate, and temperature can interact to produce size-dependent strengthening, softening, localization, and interface-mediated deformation. The smallest structure is therefore not automatically the strongest structure. Instead, performance emerges from the competition between mechanisms. The book also treats statistical heterogeneity as a fundamental nanoscale problem. A single nanoparticle, nanowire, quantum dot, or thin film may exhibit an extraordinary property. A manufactured population contains distributions. Size dispersion,shape dispersion,surface chemistry variation,defect variance,interface variation,and measurement uncertainty can reshape the realized performance of the system. For this reason, nanoscale engineering must move beyond average values. The tails of a distribution may control failure. Rare defects may dominate reliability. Measurement uncertainty may create apparent size-property relationships. And ensemble behavior may differ fundamentally from single-object behavior. This leads to a broader research principle: A nanoscale result is not mature simply because a striking property has been observed once. It becomes engineering knowledge only when the regime, mechanism, uncertainty, reproducibility, reliability, and translation pathway are understood together. The book therefore develops an emerging research direction: Nanoscale Regime Engineering. Nanoscale Regime Engineering asks whether the boundaries between physical behaviors can themselves be mapped, controlled, and used as engineering resources. Instead of treating nanoscale transitions as complications, it asks whether they can become design spaces. The core logic is: Scale→Characteristic-Length Competition→Regime Transition→Dominant Mechanism→Measurement→Control→Manufacturing→Reliability This transforms nanoscale science from the study of small objects into the engineering of physical regimes. Designed as a large-scale Living Interactive research volume, the book contains 200 Research Gates. Each Gate represents an unresolved research space rather than a final answer. The Gates extend into questions involving quantum confinement, ballistic–diffusive transitions, nanoscale heat flow, electrical boundary scattering, plasmonic response, mechanical size effects, phase stability, ensemble statistics, quantum-to-classical crossover, characteristic-length competition, measurement uncertainty, manufacturing tolerances, and regime-aware reliability. The purpose of these Research Gates is not to claim that nanoscale physics has already been completed. It is to leave structured entrances into the unknown. From size,to regime; from regime,to mechanism; from mechanism,to measurement; from measurement,to control; from control,to scalable engineering. NANOSCALE PHYSICS & SIZE-DEPENDENT TRANSPORT AT THE LIMIT ultimately proposes that the smallest scale should not be understood merely as a smaller version of the macroscopic world. At sufficiently small dimensions, the rules that dominate the system can change. The scientific task is to discover where those changes occur. The engineering task is to make them controllable. Feng Cheng-en (33) × Starli STARLI Arcane Research Edition 100K+ English200 Research GatesGoogle Books Living Interactive EditionSeptember 2026 33’s Shop of the Unknown We don’t sell certainty.We sell researchable unknowns.

BAG-DNN: A Novel Attention-Gated Deep Neural Network Ensemble for Interpretable Breast Cancer Diagnosis

Chetan P. Gondaliya, Priya R. Swaminarayan — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.47760/ijcsmc.2026.v15i09.006

Breast cancer remains one of the leading causes of cancer mortality among women worldwide, and computer-aided diagnosis (CAD) systems built on machine learning have become an important second reader for radiologists and pathologists. While deep neural networks (DNNs) can match or exceed classical classifiers on many tasks, their black-box nature limits clinical trust, and on small tabular clinical datasets they do not always outperform simpler, well-regularized models. This paper proposes an Attention-Gated Deep Neural Network (AG-DNN) for tabular breast-cancer diagnosis, in which a learnable, sigmoid-based feature-wise gate re-weights the 30 input clinical measurements before they enter a standard dense network. The gate is centered so that the model is initialized identically to a plain DNN and only learns to emphasize or suppress features where doing so improves the objective, guaranteeing that the attention mechanism can never reduce representational capacity relative to the baseline. Five independently initialized AG-DNN members are combined by probability averaging into a Bagged AG-DNN (BAG-DNN), the final proposed model. Using the Wisconsin Diagnostic Breast Cancer (WDBC) dataset (569 patients, 30 features) and stratified 5-fold cross-validation, BAG-DNN is compared against Logistic Regression, Random Forest, an RBF Support Vector Machine, a scikit-learn multilayer perceptron, a single plain DNN, and a plain-DNN ensemble. BAG-DNN improves mean cross-validated accuracy from 0.9666 (single AG-DNN) to 0.9702 and reduces the standard deviation of AUC from 0.0085 to 0.0040, reaching a mean ROC-AUC of 0.9954 — statistically indistinguishable from the strongest baselines (Logistic Regression 0.9953, MLP 0.9959) — while remaining fully interpretable: the learned attention weights consistently up-weight the ‘worst’ (most extreme) tumor-morphology measurements, in agreement with established clinical and statistical findings about this dataset. We report all results transparently, including cases where the attention mechanism does not exceed a simple ensembled plain DNN on raw accuracy, and discuss this trade-off between interpretability and accuracy gain.

Draft genome assembly and annotation of coccoid green algae Micractinium simplicissimum from Antarctica

Euna Jo — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.32685531

Micractinium simplicissimum is a coccoid green microalga first described from Antarctic freshwater habitats, with additional records from Russia. Despite the industrial potential of the genus Micractinium, genomic resources within the genus remain limited, and no nuclear genome has been reported for this species to date. Therefore, this study the first to present its de novo nuclear genome assembly. Here, we sequenced and assembled the genome and transcriptome of M. simplicissimum strain KSF0100 from Antarctica using Oxford Nanopore long reads, Illumina short reads, and RNA-seq data, and performed structural and functional gene annotation.

Cross-platform transportability of a locked immune–metabolic transcriptional score for breast cancer overall survival

Danhua He, Qiang Li — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22995292

Analysis code and derived data accompanying the manuscript “Cross-platform transportability of a locked immune–metabolic transcriptional score for breast cancer overall survival.” A score built in GSE42568 (104 tumours, 35 deaths) with frozen genes, Cox coefficients, training scaling and cutoff was applied once, without retraining or recentering, to three independent validation cohorts spanning two assay classes: GSE20685 (Affymetrix microarray, 327 tumours), SCAN-B GSE96058 (RNA-seq, 3,273 patients) and METABRIC (Illumina HT-12 microarray, 1,980 analyzable patients). The primary estimand is the continuous per-SD association from Cox regression. Headline finding: the continuous association replicated in all three cohorts (per-SD HR 1.59 [95% CI 1.29-1.98], 1.44 [1.31-1.59] and 1.13 [1.07-1.20]) while the absolute cutoff did not transport, and the exploratory five-class PAM50 heterogeneity pattern in SCAN-B was not reproduced in the broader METABRIC CLAUDIN_SUBTYPE analysis (global interaction p=0.42). Effects were time-dependent in the microarray cohorts. This archive contains the pipeline scripts, the derived result files (JSON/TSV), the figure sources and the derived data tables. It does not contain the primary expression or clinical data, which are obtained from the public accessions named in the repository README.

Analysis code for: An 8-gene prognostic signature derived from Scutellaria barbata targets predicts survival and immune microenvironment in hepatocellular carcinoma

li hongxue, li zhaoxia, liu limin — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22985408

Analysis code: 8-gene prognostic signature derived from Scutellaria barbata targets predicts survival and immune microenvironment in hepatocellular carcinoma Analysis pipeline for the manuscript: Li H, Li Z, Liu L. An 8-gene prognostic signature derived from Scutellaria barbata targets predicts survival and immune microenvironment in hepatocellular carcinoma. Frontiers in Pharmacology (submitted). The study integrates network pharmacology (TCMSP), TCGA-LIHC transcriptomics, LASSO-penalized Cox regression, bootstrap and external validation (GSE14520), immune infiltration analysis, and molecular docking (CB-Dock2). All results are computational predictions. Environment Python 3.10+ Packages: pandas, numpy, scipy, scikit-learn, lifelines, matplotlib, gseapy R 4.6.1 with packages glmnet 5.1, survival 3.8-12 (LASSO variable selection only) Install the Python dependencies with: Pipeline overview (run order) Step Script Function 1 merge_targets.py Merge predicted S. barbata targets from TCMSP outputs (241 unique targets) 2 rerun_step3_univariate.py Univariate Cox regression on the 236 expressed targets in TCGA-LIHC (67 OS-associated genes, P < 0.05) 3a lasso_glmnet.R LASSO-penalized Cox regression in R (glmnet 5.1, cv.glmnet, family = "cox", 10-fold CV, lambda.min = 0.0063) on the 236 expressed targets; selects the 8 model genes and outputs their coefficients (Figure 3A/B) 3b rerun_new_model.py Recompute RiskScore from the 8 R-derived coefficients on standardized expression; Kaplan–Meier and log-rank analyses 4 rerun_step3_multivariate_cox.py Multivariate Cox regression of RiskScore with age, gender, and stage (Table 3 of the manuscript) 5 rerun_step5_bootstrap_fixed.py Bootstrap internal validation (1,000 resamples) of the C-index 6 external_validation_new.py External validation in GSE14520 (C-index, Kaplan–Meier) 7 rerun_step4_immune.py / rerun_immune_full.py Immune infiltration differences between risk groups (11 immune features; Mann–Whitney U with FDR) 8 enrichment.py GO/KEGG enrichment of candidate targets (gseapy/Enrichr) 9 plot_figures.py Kaplan–Meier curves, time-dependent ROC, and RiskScore distribution figures 10 table1_final.py Baseline characteristics table statistics Molecular docking was performed with the CB-Dock2 web server (structures 5X02 and 7XW8); docking poses are not part of this code package. Reproducibility note on the LASSO step The original LASSO variable selection was run interactively and its cross-validation seed was not recorded. lasso_glmnet.R reconstructs the exact procedure (R 4.6.1, glmnet 5.1, cv.glmnet, family = "cox", 10-fold CV) with set.seed(123) for deterministic execution. Because the fold split is random, re-running with another seed yields a similar but not identical gene set — a well-known property of LASSO-CV. The 8-gene coefficients reported in the manuscript (Table 2; original run, lambda.min = 0.0063) are hard-coded in lasso_glmnet.R (COEF_REPORTED) and in rerun_new_model.py; all downstream results (RiskScore, C-index, Kaplan–Meier, bootstrap, external validation) are exactly reproducible from these coefficients. Data sources TCMSP (https://old.tcmsp-e.com/tcmsp.php): 29 active compounds of S. barbata (OB ≥ 30%, DL ≥ 0.18) and 241 predicted targets TCGA-LIHC (https://portal.gdc.cancer.gov/): RNA-seq and clinical data (training cohort) GSE14520 (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/geo/): external validation cohort Raw data are not redistributed here; download from the sources above and adjust the input paths at the top of each script.

FRANK: a pan-cancer RNA-seq classifier for childhood tumors with data-efficient learning

Karolis Šablauskas, Tatjana Kiseļova, Līvija Bārdiņa, Inga Nartiša, Andrius Žučenka — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.09.23.26355605

Accurate characterization of tumor subtype is of paramount importance in guiding treatment decisions in pediatric cancers. Here, we present FRANK (Fully-connected RNA-based Augmentation Neural Klassifier), a machine learning approach for classifying pan-cancer pediatric tumors. We curated a set of 11,467 transcriptomes to build a high-quality training dataset representing 181 distinct childhood tumor and normal tissue types. We used extensive data augmentation techniques with a hierarchical penalty term to train a neural network capable of detecting tumor types with as few as three training examples. To evaluate the generalization of the trained model, we perform validation on nine datasets spanning 16,398 samples, achieving an overall accuracy of 98.3%. Furthermore, we demonstrated that FRANK outperformed other published pediatric pan-cancer RNA-seq classifiers on an external dataset. To evaluate the robustness, we showed that FRANK remained accurate when applied to long-read RNA-sequencing data, as well as to noisy and low-quality data.

Analysis Code for: A Tumor-Cell Efferocytosis Program Linked to Stromal Immune Exclusion in Colorectal Cancer

Shaheryar Shafi, Arslan Hamid, Jiao Yang, Minxuan Sun — 2026-09-27 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22985254

This software record contains the R analysis scripts accompanying “A Tumor-Cell Efferocytosis Program Linked to Stromal Immune Exclusion in Colorectal Cancer.” The scripts document the dual-species RNA-seq processing and exploratory Efferocytosis Response Signature (ERS) derivation, clinical ERS scoring across TCGA-CRC, GSE39582, and GSE17536, survival and meta-analyses, immune and network analyses, ESTIMATE and CMScaller sensitivity analyses, and generation of revised figures and the 1,000-bootstrap-resample nomogram validation. The archive includes code and a README, but not raw sequencing reads or all intermediate analysis objects. Raw RNA-seq data generated for the study are deposited separately under GEO accession GSE329428; other source datasets and reference transcriptomes must be obtained from their original repositories. Some scripts document exploratory analyses rather than results presented in the main manuscript, and the archive is not a stand-alone automated workflow. Users should consult the README for input requirements, project-root paths, execution order, and software information.
